On days when nothing’s really going on at home, I’m more okay, I guess. Like, I’m not just randomly miserable all the time.

It’s more that since my sister and her baby moved back in, if something happens at the house I get on edge really fast, and then I’m already worn out by the time I get to class or the shop. Kind of like starting a shift with the machine already overheating, you know? Even if it still runs, it’s not running right.

So if the house is quiet, my mood’s not terrible. I’m still getting by. But the stress there has been making me snap over little stuff, and then I end up drained and not keeping up as well—missed a couple deadlines, and I backed out of helping with the theater set because I just couldn’t keep everything straight.

Yeah, I mean… I wouldn’t say it comes out of nowhere exactly. It’s more like since my sister and her baby moved back home, I’m kind of already braced for something to happen, you know?

Yeah, kind of. I mean, I don’t know if it’s “most days” exactly, but I’ve been showing up to class or the shop already drained a lot more than usual.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know… I haven’t really thought about it like that.

Like, I still like the stuff I like, I guess. If I’m hanging out with friends or working on something, it can still be good. Sometimes I’m distracted, though, and then I’m not really in it. But that’s also like—my friend was asking me to go out the other night and I was thinking about this whole other thing with my tools at the shop, because somebody keeps putting them back in the wrong drawer, which drives me nuts.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if it’s that things aren’t enjoyable anymore. I don’t think it’s that, exactly. It’s more like I’m not always able to settle into them.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed that, even when you’re not distracted or stressed, the things you usually enjoy don’t feel as satisfying or rewarding as they used to?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 18 / industrial_or_refractory_machinery_mechanic / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed it that cleanly, like “even when nothing’s going on, this isn’t satisfying anymore.”

Usually if I’m not enjoying something, there’s some other thing pulling at me, or I’m thinking about something from earlier. Like even with stuff I normally like, I’ll start thinking about whether I left something in the wrong place at the shop, and then I’m only half paying attention. But if I actually get into it, it still feels good, I think.

So… no, I haven’t really noticed that happening on its own.

It’s more like I’m not always able to settle into them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that, even when you’re not distracted or stressed, the things you usually enjoy don’t feel as satisfying or rewarding as they used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed it that cleanly, like “even when nothing’s going on, this isn’t satisfying anymore.”\n\nUsually if I’m not enjoying something, there’s some other thing pulling at me, or I’m thinking about something from earlier. Like even with stuff I normally like, I’ll start thinking about whether I left something in the wrong place at the shop, and then I’m only half paying attention. But if I actually get into it, it still feels good, I think.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that happening on its own.\n\n[Last Question]\nEven when you’re not distracted or stressed, do you find that activities you usually enjoy feel less satisfying or rewarding than they used to?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 18 / industrial_or_refractory_machinery_mechanic / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I mean… I don’t think so. Or I haven’t noticed it that way.

Like, if I actually get to do something without my head getting yanked somewhere else, it can still be satisfying. It’s just kind of hard to tell because there’s usually something else happening, you know? Like I’ll sit down to do something I normally like and then remember some dumb thing from earlier, and suddenly I’m not really paying attention.

But if you mean, like, the activity itself feels flat even when I’m fine otherwise… I don’t think that’s been happening.

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you found that things you usually enjoy, like your favorite activities or time with friends, don’t feel as rewarding or pleasurable as they used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really thought about it like that.\n\nLike, I still like the stuff I like, I guess. I don’t think it’s that, exactly. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   {
    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t really think so. I mean, not that I’ve noticed in that specific way.

It’s more like if I’m distracted, then yeah, I’m not getting much out of it, but that’s not the same thing as the thing itself feeling worse. Like, if I’m actually focused on it, it still seems fine. I don’t know, maybe I’m overthinking the difference, but… no, I haven’t really noticed it being less rewarding just on its own.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I’ve noticed that, no. Like if I’m actually doing something I like and I’m not pulled off into thinking about other stuff, it still can feel good.

I guess sometimes I start judging it, like “am I enjoying this enough?” which is kind of a weird thing to do, and then that ruins it a little. But that’s more me getting in my own head. Same thing used to happen in school when I’d try to relax and then remember some assignment, or even now I’ll be watching something and think about a tool I forgot to clean properly. But if I’m really just there with it, I don’t think it feels less rewarding than before.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean, I don’t think so. Not in that clean way you’re asking.

Like, if I’m actually not distracted and not already kind of wound up, then stuff I enjoy still feels… basically enjoyable. Sometimes I get sidetracked thinking about whether I should be doing something else, which is annoying, but that’s different. It’s like when you’re trying to watch a video and the audio is fine, but there’s someone talking in the background, so you can’t really tell if the video is boring or if you’re just not listening.

So… no, I haven’t really noticed the activities themselves feeling less satisfying.

Like, I still want to do stuff, I just get distracted or end up not starting it. \n\nI mean, I was supposed to help my friend with this little project for class, and then I got thinking about how the shop teacher used to say you can’t fix a machine if you don’t know what sound it’s making first—which is kind of true, but also annoying because sometimes everything sounds broken, you know? \n\nBut yeah, I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear “I don’t enjoy things anymore” thing. And by the time I get to class or the shop, I’m already drained. I’m still doing stuff, mostly, but I’ve missed a couple deadlines and I had to back out of helping with the theater set because I just couldn’t keep everything straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or like you’re not good enough, even when things aren’t really your fault?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really in a big way, I guess. I mean, I feel bad after I snap at people, like I’ll replay it and think, “Why did I say it like that?” But I don’t think I walk around feeling like everything’s my fault or like I’m just not good enough.\n\nIt’s more like… you know when you’re trying to put a part back together and one little screw keeps rolling off the bench, and suddenly you’re mad at the screw even though it’s just gravity? It’s kind of like that. I feel dumb afterward, but I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what your mood is like on days when nothing particularly stressful or upsetting is happening at home?\n\nPatient: On days when nothing’s really going on at home, I’m more okay, I guess. Like, I’m not just randomly miserable all the time.\n\nIt’s more that since my sister and her baby moved back in, if something happens at the house I get on edge really fast, and then I’m already worn out by the time I get to class or the shop. Kind of like starting a shift with the machine already overheating, you know? Even if it still runs, it’s not running right.\n\nSo if the house is quiet, my mood’s not terrible. I’m still getting by. But the stress there has been making me snap over little stuff, and then I end up drained and not keeping up as well—missed a couple deadlines, and I backed out of helping with the theater set because I just couldn’t keep everything straight.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot or feeling tense, even when there’s nothing specific that seems to be causing it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I wouldn’t say it comes out of nowhere exactly. It’s more like since my sister and her baby moved back home, I’m kind of already braced for something to happen, you know?\n\nLike I’ll be at the house and some tiny thing sets me off, and then I get to class or the shop already worn out. It’s stupid stuff sometimes—like noise or things being moved around—and I’ll snap, then I’m thinking about it later instead of focusing. I’m still getting through most things, but I’ve missed a couple deadlines, and I backed out of helping with the theater set because I just couldn’t keep everything straight.\n\nSo… yeah, tense, but mostly tied to all that home stuff.\n\nDoctor: Even on days when things at home are calm and nothing stressful is going on, do you still find yourself feeling down or low in mood for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Not really for most of the day, no. If things at home are actually calm, I’m not just sitting there feeling low all day for no reason.\n\nIt’s more like… ever since my sister and the baby moved back in, I’m on edge more than I used to be. Then I snap over some little thing at the house, and by the time I get to class or the shop I’m already drained. Like I’m still doing stuff, I’m not completely falling apart, but I’ve missed a couple deadlines and I backed out of helping with the theater set because I just couldn’t keep everything straight.\n\nSo I do feel more down than usual sometimes, but it feels tied to all that, not like it’s there all day no matter what.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling tired or lacking energy most days, even when you haven’t done anything physically exhausting?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. I mean, I don’t know if it’s “most days” exactly, but I’ve been showing up to class or the shop already drained a lot more than usual.\n\nIt’s like ever since my sister and her baby moved back home, the house stuff gets me wound up before I even leave. I’ll snap over some little thing, and then I’m carrying that with me the rest of the day. Like, by the time I’m supposed to focus on a deadline or whatever, my brain’s already tired.\n\nI’m still getting by, but not as well. I missed a couple deadlines, and I ended up backing out of helping with the theater set because I just couldn’t keep everything straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that things you usually enjoy, like your favorite activities or time with friends, don’t feel as rewarding or pleasurable as they used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really thought about it like that.\n\nLike, I still like the stuff I like, I guess. If I’m hanging out with friends or working on something, it can still be good. Sometimes I’m distracted, though, and then I’m not really in it. But that’s also like—my friend was asking me to go out the other night and I was thinking about this whole other thing with my tools at the shop, because somebody keeps putting them back in the wrong drawer, which drives me nuts.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if it’s that things aren’t enjoyable anymore. I don’t think it’s that, exactly. It’s more like I’m not always able to settle into them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that, even when you’re not distracted or stressed, the things you usually enjoy don’t feel as satisfying or rewarding as they used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed it that cleanly, like “even when nothing’s going on, this isn’t satisfying anymore.”\n\nUsually if I’m not enjoying something, there’s some other thing pulling at me, or I’m thinking about something from earlier. Like even with stuff I normally like, I’ll start thinking about whether I left something in the wrong place at the shop, and then I’m only half paying attention. But if I actually get into it, it still feels good, I think.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that happening on its own.\n\nDoctor: Even when you’re not distracted or stressed, do you find that activities you usually enjoy feel less satisfying or rewarding than they used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I don’t think so. Like, if I’m actually focused on it, it still seems fine. I don’t know, maybe I’m overthinking the difference, but… no, I haven’t really noticed it being less rewarding just on its own.\n\nDoctor: Even when you're not distracted or stressed, do you find that activities you usually enjoy feel less satisfying or rewarding than they used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed that, no. Like if I’m actually doing something I like and I’m not pulled off into thinking about other stuff, it still can feel good.\n\nI guess sometimes I start judging it, like “am I enjoying this enough?” which is kind of a weird thing to do, and then that ruins it a little. But if I’m really just there with it, I don’t think it feels less rewarding than before.\n\nDoctor: Even when you're not distracted or stressed, have you found that activities you usually enjoy feel less satisfying or rewarding than they used to?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don’t think so. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I can really say that, no. Like, if I’m actually calm and paying attention, something I like still feels pretty much like itself.

It’s more that I’ll drift off in my head, or start comparing it to how I “should” be feeling, which is kind of dumb because then I’m not even just doing the thing anymore. Like with music, sometimes I’ll be listening and then I start thinking about whether the speaker sounds off, or whether I need to fix something at work, and suddenly I’m not enjoying it—but that feels more like I got pulled away from it, not that the music stopped being good.

So… yeah, I haven’t really noticed it being less rewarding when I’m actually present for it.

Like, I still want to do stuff, I just get distracted or end up not starting it. \n\nI mean, I was supposed to help my friend with this little project for class, and then I got thinking about how the shop teacher used to say you can’t fix a machine if you don’t know what sound it’s making first—which is kind of true, but also annoying because sometimes everything sounds broken, you know? \n\nBut yeah, I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear “I don’t enjoy things anymore” thing. It’s more like I don’t always get around to them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down or having a low mood most days, even when there’s no obvious reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. I mean, it’s not like I wake up sad for no reason every single day, but since my sister and the baby moved back in, I’ve been more down and on edge than I’m used to.\n\nIt’s like I’ll be fine, then some little thing at home sets me off, and I snap, and then I feel bad about it. And by the time I get to class or the shop, I’m already drained. I’m still doing stuff, mostly, but I’ve missed a couple deadlines and I had to back out of helping with the theater set because I just couldn’t keep everything straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or like you’re not good enough, even when things aren’t really your fault?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really in a big way, I guess. I mean, I feel bad after I snap at people, like I’ll replay it and think, “Why did I say it like that?” But I don’t think I walk around feeling like everything’s my fault or like I’m just not good enough.\n\nIt’s more like… you know when you’re trying to put a part back together and one little screw keeps rolling off the bench, and suddenly you’re mad at the screw even though it’s just gravity? It’s kind of like that. I feel dumb afterward, but I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what your mood is like on days when nothing particularly stressful or upsetting is happening at home?\n\nPatient: On days when nothing’s really going on at home, I’m more okay, I guess. Like, I’m not just randomly miserable all the time.\n\nIt’s more that since my sister and her baby moved back in, if something happens at the house I get on edge really fast, and then I’m already worn out by the time I get to class or the shop. Kind of like starting a shift with the machine already overheating, you know? Even if it still runs, it’s not running right.\n\nSo if the house is quiet, my mood’s not terrible. I’m still getting by. But the stress there has been making me snap over little stuff, and then I end up drained and not keeping up as well—missed a couple deadlines, and I backed out of helping with the theater set because I just couldn’t keep everything straight.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot or feeling tense, even when there’s nothing specific that seems to be causing it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I wouldn’t say it comes out of nowhere exactly. It’s more like since my sister and her baby moved back home, I’m kind of already braced for something to happen, you know?\n\nLike I’ll be at the house and some tiny thing sets me off, and then I get to class or the shop already worn out. It’s stupid stuff sometimes—like noise or things being moved around—and I’ll snap, then I’m thinking about it later instead of focusing. I’m still getting through most things, but I’ve missed a couple deadlines, and I backed out of helping with the theater set because I just couldn’t keep everything straight.\n\nSo… yeah, tense, but mostly tied to all that home stuff.\n\nDoctor: Even on days when things at home are calm and nothing stressful is going on, do you still find yourself feeling down or low in mood for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Not really for most of the day, no. If things at home are actually calm, I’m not just sitting there feeling low all day for no reason.\n\nIt’s more like… ever since my sister and the baby moved back in, I’m on edge more than I used to be. Then I snap over some little thing at the house, and by the time I get to class or the shop I’m already drained. Like I’m still doing stuff, I’m not completely falling apart, but I’ve missed a couple deadlines and I backed out of helping with the theater set because I just couldn’t keep everything straight.\n\nSo I do feel more down than usual sometimes, but it feels tied to all that, not like it’s there all day no matter what.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling tired or lacking energy most days, even when you haven’t done anything physically exhausting?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. I mean, I don’t know if it’s “most days” exactly, but I’ve been showing up to class or the shop already drained a lot more than usual.\n\nIt’s like ever since my sister and her baby moved back home, the house stuff gets me wound up before I even leave. I’ll snap over some little thing, and then I’m carrying that with me the rest of the day. Like, by the time I’m supposed to focus on a deadline or whatever, my brain’s already tired.\n\nI’m still getting by, but not as well. I missed a couple deadlines, and I ended up backing out of helping with the theater set because I just couldn’t keep everything straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that things you usually enjoy, like your favorite activities or time with friends, don’t feel as rewarding or pleasurable as they used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really thought about it like that.\n\nLike, I still like the stuff I like, I guess. If I’m hanging out with friends or working on something, it can still be good. Sometimes I’m distracted, though, and then I’m not really in it. But that’s also like—my friend was asking me to go out the other night and I was thinking about this whole other thing with my tools at the shop, because somebody keeps putting them back in the wrong drawer, which drives me nuts.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if it’s that things aren’t enjoyable anymore. I don’t think it’s that, exactly. It’s more like I’m not always able to settle into them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that, even when you’re not distracted or stressed, the things you usually enjoy don’t feel as satisfying or rewarding as they used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed it that cleanly, like “even when nothing’s going on, this isn’t satisfying anymore.”\n\nUsually if I’m not enjoying something, there’s some other thing pulling at me, or I’m thinking about something from earlier. Like even with stuff I normally like, I’ll start thinking about whether I left something in the wrong place at the shop, and then I’m only half paying attention. But if I actually get into it, it still feels good, I think.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that happening on its own.\n\nDoctor: Even when you’re not distracted or stressed, do you find that activities you usually enjoy feel less satisfying or rewarding than they used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I don’t think so. Or I haven’t noticed it that way.\n\nLike, if I actually get to do something without my head getting yanked somewhere else, it can still be satisfying. It’s just kind of hard to tell because there’s usually something else happening, you know? Like I’ll sit down to do something I normally like and then remember some dumb thing from earlier, and suddenly I’m not really paying attention.\n\nBut if you mean, like, the activity itself feels flat even when I’m fine otherwise… I don’t think that’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that, even when you’re not distracted or stressed, the things you usually enjoy feel less satisfying or rewarding than they used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think so. I mean, not that I’ve noticed in that specific way.\n\nIt’s more like if I’m distracted, then yeah, I’m not getting much out of it, but that’s not the same thing as the thing itself feeling worse. Like, if I’m actually focused on it, it still seems fine. I don’t know, maybe I’m overthinking the difference, but… no, I haven’t really noticed it being less rewarding just on its own.\n\nDoctor: Even when you're not distracted or stressed, do you find that activities you usually enjoy feel less satisfying or rewarding than they used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed that, no. Like if I’m actually doing something I like and I’m not pulled off into thinking about other stuff, it still can feel good.\n\nI guess sometimes I start judging it, like “am I enjoying this enough?” which is kind of a weird thing to do, and then that ruins it a little. But that’s more me getting in my own head. Same thing used to happen in school when I’d try to relax and then remember some assignment, or even now I’ll be watching something and think about a tool I forgot to clean properly. But if I’m really just there with it, I don’t think it feels less rewarding than before.\n\nDoctor: Even when you're not distracted or stressed, have you found that activities you usually enjoy feel less satisfying or rewarding than they used to?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don’t think so. Not in that clean way you’re asking.\n\nLike, if I’m actually not distracted and not already kind of wound up, then stuff I enjoy still feels… basically enjoyable. Sometimes I get sidetracked thinking about whether I should be doing something else, which is annoying, but that’s different. It’s like when you’re trying to watch a video and the audio is fine, but there’s someone talking in the background, so you can’t really tell if the video is boring or if you’re just not listening.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed the activities themselves feeling less satisfying.\n\nDoctor: Even when you're not distracted or stressed, do you find that activities you usually enjoy feel less satisfying or rewarding than they used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can really say that, no. Like, if I’m actually calm and paying attention, something I like still feels pretty much like itself.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll drift off in my head, or start comparing it to how I “should” be feeling, which is kind of dumb because then I’m not even just doing the thing anymore. Like with music, sometimes I’ll be listening and then I start thinking about whether the speaker sounds off, or whether I need to fix something at work, and suddenly I’m not enjoying it—but that feels more like I got pulled away from it, not that the music stopped being good.\n\nSo… yeah, I haven’t really noticed it being less rewarding when I’m actually present for it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Yeah, I mean… I think I already kind of answered it, but no, not really. If I’m actually relaxed and paying attention, I don’t notice the thing itself feeling worse or less worth doing.

But if you mean the enjoyment is just gone even when nothing else is interfering… I haven’t really noticed that.

I missed a couple deadlines, and I ended up backing out of helping with the theater set because I just couldn’t keep everything straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that things you usually enjoy, like your favorite activities or time with friends, don’t feel as rewarding or pleasurable as they used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really thought about it like that.\n\nLike, I still like the stuff I like, I guess. If I’m hanging out with friends or working on something, it can still be good. Sometimes I’m distracted, though, and then I’m not really in it. But that’s also like—my friend was asking me to go out the other night and I was thinking about this whole other thing with my tools at the shop, because somebody keeps putting them back in the wrong drawer, which drives me nuts.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if it’s that things aren’t enjoyable anymore. I don’t think it’s that, exactly. It’s more like I’m not always able to settle into them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that, even when you’re not distracted or stressed, the things you usually enjoy don’t feel as satisfying or rewarding as they used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed it that cleanly, like “even when nothing’s going on, this isn’t satisfying anymore.”\n\nUsually if I’m not enjoying something, there’s some other thing pulling at me, or I’m thinking about something from earlier. Like even with stuff I normally like, I’ll start thinking about whether I left something in the wrong place at the shop, and then I’m only half paying attention. But if I actually get into it, it still feels good, I think.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that happening on its own.\n\nDoctor: Even when you’re not distracted or stressed, do you find that activities you usually enjoy feel less satisfying or rewarding than they used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I don’t think so. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess my answer’s still basically no. I haven’t really noticed that happening when I’m actually just doing the thing and not all in my head about it.

Like, sometimes I’ll think something should feel more exciting or whatever, but then I’m sort of judging it instead of enjoying it. It’s like when I’m working on a machine and you start listening for one weird sound, and then suddenly every normal sound seems suspicious… not because the machine changed, just because you’re focused on it weirdly.

So, yeah, I don’t think the enjoyment is actually gone or less rewarding on its own.

It’s kind of like that. I feel dumb afterward, but I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what your mood is like on days when nothing particularly stressful or upsetting is happening at home?\n\nPatient: On days when nothing’s really going on at home, I’m more okay, I guess. Like, I’m not just randomly miserable all the time.\n\nIt’s more that since my sister and her baby moved back in, if something happens at the house I get on edge really fast, and then I’m already worn out by the time I get to class or the shop. Like I’m still doing stuff, I’m not completely falling apart, but I’ve missed a couple deadlines and I backed out of helping with the theater set because I just couldn’t keep everything straight.\n\nSo I do feel more down than usual sometimes, but it feels tied to all that, not like it’s there all day no matter what.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling tired or lacking energy most days, even when you haven’t done anything physically exhausting?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. I mean, I don’t know if it’s “most days” exactly, but I’ve been showing up to class or the shop already drained a lot more than usual.\n\nIt’s like ever since my sister and her baby moved back home, the house stuff gets me wound up before I even leave. I’ll snap over some little thing, and then I’m carrying that with me the rest of the day. Like, by the time I’m supposed to focus on a deadline or whatever, my brain’s already tired.\n\nI’m still getting by, but not as well. I missed a couple deadlines, and I ended up backing out of helping with the theater set because I just couldn’t keep everything straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that things you usually enjoy, like your favorite activities or time with friends, don’t feel as rewarding or pleasurable as they used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really thought about it like that.\n\nLike, I still like the stuff I like, I guess. If I’m hanging out with friends or working on something, it can still be good. Sometimes I’m distracted, though, and then I’m not really in it. But that’s also like—my friend was asking me to go out the other night and I was thinking about this whole other thing with my tools at the shop, because somebody keeps putting them back in the wrong drawer, which drives me nuts.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if it’s that things aren’t enjoyable anymore. I don’t think it’s that, exactly. It’s more like I’m not always able to settle into them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that, even when you’re not distracted or stressed, the things you usually enjoy don’t feel as satisfying or rewarding as they used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed it that cleanly, like “even when nothing’s going on, this isn’t satisfying anymore.”\n\nUsually if I’m not enjoying something, there’s some other thing pulling at me, or I’m thinking about something from earlier. Like even with stuff I normally like, I’ll start thinking about whether I left something in the wrong place at the shop, and then I’m only half paying attention. But if I actually get into it, it still feels good, I think.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that happening on its own.\n\nDoctor: Even when you’re not distracted or stressed, do you find that activities you usually enjoy feel less satisfying or rewarding than they used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I don’t think so. Or I haven’t noticed it that way.\n\nLike, if I actually get to do something without my head getting yanked somewhere else, it can still be satisfying. It’s just kind of hard to tell because there’s usually something else happening, you know? Like I’ll sit down to do something I normally like and then remember some dumb thing from earlier, and suddenly I’m not really paying attention.\n\nBut if you mean, like, the activity itself feels flat even when I’m fine otherwise… I don’t think that’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that, even when you’re not distracted or stressed, the things you usually enjoy feel less satisfying or rewarding than they used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think so. I mean, not that I’ve noticed in that specific way.\n\nIt’s more like if I’m distracted, then yeah, I’m not getting much out of it, but that’s not the same thing as the thing itself feeling worse. Like, if I’m actually focused on it, it still seems fine. I don’t know, maybe I’m overthinking the difference, but… no, I haven’t really noticed it being less rewarding just on its own.\n\nDoctor: Even when you're not distracted or stressed, do you find that activities you usually enjoy feel less satisfying or rewarding than they used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed that, no. Like if I’m actually doing something I like and I’m not pulled off into thinking about other stuff, it still can feel good.\n\nI guess sometimes I start judging it, like “am I enjoying this enough?” which is kind of a weird thing to do, and then that ruins it a little. But that’s more me getting in my own head. Same thing used to happen in school when I’d try to relax and then remember some assignment, or even now I’ll be watching something and think about a tool I forgot to clean properly. But if I’m really just there with it, I don’t think it feels less rewarding than before.\n\nDoctor: Even when you're not distracted or stressed, have you found that activities you usually enjoy feel less satisfying or rewarding than they used to?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don’t think so. Not in that clean way you’re asking.\n\nLike, if I’m actually not distracted and not already kind of wound up, then stuff I enjoy still feels… basically enjoyable. Sometimes I get sidetracked thinking about whether I should be doing something else, which is annoying, but that’s different. It’s like when you’re trying to watch a video and the audio is fine, but there’s someone talking in the background, so you can’t really tell if the video is boring or if you’re just not listening.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed the activities themselves feeling less satisfying.\n\nDoctor: Even when you're not distracted or stressed, do you find that activities you usually enjoy feel less satisfying or rewarding than they used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can really say that, no. Like, if I’m actually calm and paying attention, something I like still feels pretty much like itself.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll drift off in my head, or start comparing it to how I “should” be feeling, which is kind of dumb because then I’m not even just doing the thing anymore. Like with music, sometimes I’ll be listening and then I start thinking about whether the speaker sounds off, or whether I need to fix something at work, and suddenly I’m not enjoying it—but that feels more like I got pulled away from it, not that the music stopped being good.\n\nSo… yeah, I haven’t really noticed it being less rewarding when I’m actually present for it.\n\nDoctor: Even when you're not distracted or stressed, do you find that activities you usually enjoy feel less satisfying or rewarding than they used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I think I already kind of answered it, but no, not really. If I’m actually relaxed and paying attention, I don’t notice the thing itself feeling worse or less worth doing.\n\nI can get in my own way with it, like start thinking, “Shouldn’t this be more fun?” and then I’m not really doing the activity anymore, I’m just analyzing it. It’s like when you’re trying to watch a video and the audio is fine, but there’s someone talking in the background, so you can’t really tell if the video is boring or if you’re just not listening.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed the activities themselves feeling less satisfying.\n\nDoctor: Even when you're not distracted or stressed, do you find that activities you usually enjoy feel less satisfying or rewarding than they used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can really say that, no. Like, if I’m actually calm and paying attention, something I like still feels pretty much like itself.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll drift off in my head, or start comparing it to how I “should” be feeling, which is kind of dumb because then I’m not even just doing the thing anymore. Like with music, sometimes I’ll be listening and then I start thinking about whether the speaker sounds off, or whether I need to fix something at work, and suddenly I’m not enjoying it—but that feels more like I got pulled away from it, not that the music stopped being good.\n\nSo… yeah, I haven’t really noticed it being less rewarding when I’m actually present for it.\n\nDoctor: Even when you're not distracted or stressed, do you find that activities you usually enjoy feel less satisfying or rewarding than they used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I think I already kind of answered it, but no, not really. If I’m actually relaxed and paying attention, I don’t notice the thing itself feeling worse or less worth doing.\n\nI can get in my own way with it, like start thinking, “Shouldn’t this be more fun?” and then I’m not really doing the activity anymore, I’m just analyzing it. That happens with little stuff too, like I’ll be trying to enjoy a song and then suddenly I’m thinking about some random machine noise at work, or whether I left something out, and then I lose the moment.\n\nBut if you mean the enjoyment is just gone even when nothing else is interfering… I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Even when you're not distracted or stressed, do you find that activities you usually enjoy feel less satisfying or rewarding than they used to?\n\nPatient: I guess my answer’s still basically no. I haven’t really noticed that happening when I’m actually just doing the thing and not all in my head about it.\n\nLike, sometimes I’ll think something should feel more exciting or whatever, but then I’m sort of judging it instead of enjoying it. It’s like when I’m working on a machine and you start listening for one weird sound, and then suddenly every normal sound seems suspicious… not because the machine changed, just because you’re focused on it weirdly.\n\nSo, yeah, I don’t think the enjoyment is actually gone or less rewarding on its own."
